# System Architecture

A plain-English guide to how the system is built, what each part does, and how they connect.

---

## The big picture

Think of this system as a trading desk with several specialists who each do one job:

1. **Data Collector** — connects to Interactive Brokers and fetches live prices, option chains, and your account positions.
2. **Analysts** — calculate whether now is a good time to sell options on a stock (IV rank, technical trend, fundamentals, liquidity).
3. **Strategy Team** — generates specific trade candidates: "sell the AAPL July 200 call for $1.50".
4. **Risk Manager** — checks every candidate against hard rules before anything can proceed.
5. **Claude** — reviews the top candidates and writes a plain-English explanation of the tradeoffs.
6. **Telegram** — sends the candidates to your phone and collects your Approve/Reject response.
7. **Executor** — once you approve, re-checks the rules one more time with a live quote, then places the order.
8. **Intraday Watch** — monitors open positions during the day and alerts you when a roll is needed.

**You are the final decision-maker.** No order is ever placed without your explicit approval.

---

## How a trade happens (the pipeline)

```
IBKR live data
    ↓
Analytics (IV rank, technicals, fundamentals, liquidity)
    ↓
Strategy modules (generate candidate trades)
    ↓
Decision Engine (score + rank candidates)
    ↓
Rules Engine ← HARD STOP: enforces all risk limits, no AI involved
    ↓
Claude review (plain-English explanation, optional enrichment)
    ↓
Telegram → YOU approve or reject
    ↓
Rules Engine (re-checks with a fresh live quote) ← SECOND HARD STOP
    ↓
Order placed at IBKR (limit order at mid-price)
    ↓
Fill confirmation sent back to Telegram
```

Every step writes its results to the database. If any step fails, the system can resume from where
it left off. If Claude is unavailable, the pipeline continues without it — Claude enriches but
never blocks.

---

### The universe — what it watches

**How it works.** `config/universe.yaml` lists three overlapping sets of tickers: a **watchlist**
(names it scans and will sell covered calls against *if you already own them*), a **`would_own`**
list (names it's allowed to sell cash-secured puts on, because if assigned it's genuinely fine to
end up holding the stock), and an **`actively_wheeling`** subset of `would_own` — the core rotation
it scans every 15-minute cycle (GOOGL, NVDA, AMZN, MAGS, BAC, META, PLTR, HOOD, SOFI, HIMS,
NBIS, ASTS, RKLB, RGTI, MARA, TQQQ, UPRO, SOXL, DPST). Everything else in `would_own` ("dip-watch" —
sector ETFs like XLV/XLP, SPY, QQQ, AAPL/MSFT/JPM, and more) isn't scanned every cycle at all — it's only
pulled into a scan when its price *drops* 3%+, since a rally is never a reason to sell a new put on
a name outside the core rotation. Tickers are also grouped into three risk tiers for reference —
**safe bets**, **moderate**, and **risky** — independent of the `actively_wheeling`/dip-watch split.
Leveraged ETFs are call-only and never `would_own` **except TQQQ/UPRO/SOXL/DPST**, a deliberate,
confirmed exception — the account accepts daily-decay risk on assignment for those four
specifically (DPST joined this exception 2026-09-29). AMD is watchlist-only (CC income against an
existing 300-share position) and deliberately not in `would_own` — no CSPs. Tickers ruled out
entirely (never traded) live in `config/universe_archive.yaml`, outside the app's read path.

**In plain English.** This is the shopping list, in three tiers of eagerness. One column says
"stocks we'll write calls against if we already own them." The next says "stocks we're actually OK
ending up owning if a put gets exercised" — but within that list, a smaller "actively wheeling"
core gets checked constantly, while the rest only gets a second look after a real dip, so the
system isn't burning IBKR requests re-checking option chains on names that haven't moved. A
leveraged ETF is normally kept off the would-own list entirely — nobody wants to wake up owning a
3x-leveraged fund — except the four named above, where that tradeoff was made on purpose.

### Scanning — gathering fresh data every 15 minutes

**How it works.** During market hours a loop wakes up every 15 minutes, clock-aligned to ET
quarter-hours (9:30, 9:45, ...). Rather than re-pulling every option chain from IBKR every single
cycle (which would exhaust the ~100 concurrent market-data-line limit IBKR allows), an **intraday
materiality gate** only re-fetches a name's option chain when something about it changed: it moved
past its bucket's threshold since its **last fetch** (0.5% either way for `actively_wheeling`, a 2%
**rally** for a held stock, a 3% **drop** for dip-watch — a symbol in two buckets is tested against
both and any one firing is enough), or it cleared the score floor last cycle. A full sweep is
guaranteed on the first cycle after every process (re)start, and each symbol is force-refreshed
once **its own** last fetch is over 120 min old as a staleness safety net. A manual `/scan` from
Telegram and the startup full sweep fetch `actively_wheeling` ∪ held names unconditionally, but
dip_watch names are **seed-only** (yfinance baseline persisted, no chain fetch) unless they gapped
≥3% overnight — a gap in either direction is a legitimate CSP setup at the open. Behind the
scenes, daily price history is stored in SQLite and only the missing days are re-fetched from
Yahoo Finance, so the system doesn't re-download a year of bars every run.

**In plain English.** Every 15 minutes, it checks in on your positions and the watchlist — but
intelligently, only pulling fresh option prices for things that actually moved or that you own,
instead of hammering the broker's servers with the same requests over and over. You can also force a
full, fresh look any time by typing `/scan`.

### The analyst bench — IV, technicals, fundamentals, liquidity, sentiment, and the macro backdrop

**How it works.** For every symbol under consideration, several independent calculations run:
**IV Rank/Percentile** (is implied volatility high or low relative to its own past year?), **VRP** —
Volatility Risk Premium (current IV minus 30-day realized volatility; positive means the options
market is pricing in more movement than the stock has actually been making, which is the edge a
premium seller is paid for), an **IV/RV ratio** gate (rejects candidates where that richness isn't
present), **technicals** (RSI, MACD, moving averages, ATR, support/resistance levels, and a
trending-up/down/sideways regime classifier), **fundamentals** (free cash flow, debt, dividend
safety, next earnings date), **liquidity** (bid/ask spread, open interest, and volume — filtering out
options too thin to actually trade), a **composite sentiment score** (StockTwits self-tagged
bullish/bearish posts, recent news headlines, and optional Reddit, all scored with a lexicon-based
tone analyzer, blended into one 0–100 number), and a **macro backdrop** computed once per scan — VIX
and its term structure, the 10-year Treasury yield, SPY's recent tape, and broad-market headline
tone.

**In plain English.** This is the research team. Before anyone proposes a trade, they check: is
volatility "expensive" right now compared to how much the stock actually moves? Is the stock trending
or choppy? Are the company's finances solid? Can you actually get in and out of this option without
getting a bad price? What's the crowd's mood on this stock, and what's the overall market doing? None
of this makes a decision by itself — it just feeds the next steps.

### Fair value — the ideal zone

**How it works.** For each stock, `analytics/fair_value.py` computes an **expected move**
(spot price × IV × √(days-to-expiry/365)), then places an **ideal strike band** inside that move,
snapped toward the stock's own support/resistance levels, widened around earnings, and floored at
cost basis for covered calls. It also computes a **minimum credit** — the Black-Scholes fair value
of the option at *realized* (actual, historical) volatility, plus a required edge on top — making the
"am I being paid enough for this risk" question explicit and numeric, rather than a vibe.

**In plain English.** This is the price tag the system writes for itself before shopping. "A put
around here, for at least this much money, is a fair deal — anything cheaper isn't worth the risk."
Every contract actually offered gets compared against that tag.

### Generating trade ideas

**How it works.** Four generators turn the analysis into concrete, priced trades: **covered calls**
(for stocks you already own, find the best strike/expiry to sell against them), **cash-secured puts**
(for `would_own` names, find strikes that pay well without excessive assignment risk — sized to
however much room your account actually has left, not the maximum lot you could theoretically
afford), **rolling** (for existing short options nearing expiry or drifting in-the-money, propose the
best replacement trade), and **buy-to-own** (stocks worth buying specifically so you can write
covered calls against them later, filtered down to only the strongest handful). Every generator keeps
a record of contracts it *considered and rejected*, tagged with every reason it failed — so a quiet
scan still shows its work instead of going silent.

**In plain English.** This is where "sell the AAPL July $200 call for $1.50" actually gets written
down. Nothing here decides whether the trade is allowed — it just proposes candidates, and even the
rejects are kept around so you can see what was looked at and why it didn't make the cut.

### Scoring and ranking

**How it works.** Each candidate gets normalized 0–100 scores across IV rank, technicals,
fundamentals, liquidity, and sentiment, blended by configurable weights (`config/scoring_weights.yaml`)
into one number. Candidates below a minimum score floor, or that lose out to a better strike on the
same underlying, are dropped — and *why* each one was dropped ("a better strike on this name already
took the slot" vs. "the batch was full") is recorded rather than silently vanishing.

**In plain English.** Once you have several plausible trades, you need to rank them. This step scores
and sorts them so the best few rise to the top — and keeps a note of why anything that didn't make
the cut got cut.

### Position sizing and risk — the rulebook

**How it works.** This is the one part of the system with **no AI involved at all** — pure,
deterministic Python (`src/engine/risk_engine.py`), and the *only* path to an order. It enforces,
cumulatively across the whole account, not just one trade at a time:

- **Cash feasibility** — a reserve (the greater of 20% of available cash or a $10,000 floor) is set
  aside first; cash-secured puts may only use what's left ("deployable cash").
- **Concentration, measured in risk units** (`collateral × IV × √(days-to-expiry/365)`, not raw dollar
  size — so a $65,000 position in a calm stock and a $15,000 position in a wild one can be compared
  fairly). Per-ticker and per-sector caps apply.
- **Large-position slots** — at most two positions at a time are allowed to exceed the standard
  per-ticker cap (each up to a hard ceiling), so a couple of deliberate, high-conviction bets are
  possible without the whole book turning into a pile of oversized bets.
- **The income gate** — a candidate must offer a premium above its computed fair value plus an edge
  (the VRP floor above); a couple of very low bars on return-on-capital and annualized yield exist
  only to filter out obvious noise, not as the primary test.
- Plus IV-rank and IV/RV-ratio floors, a DTE (days-to-expiry) window, a required delta range, and an
  earnings-date blackout.

Every check runs **twice**: once when the candidate is ranked, and again right before the order is
sent, against a fresh live quote — so a stale price can't slip a bad trade through.

**In plain English.** This is the compliance officer who can't be talked out of anything — not even
by Claude. However good an idea sounds, if it breaks a rule (too much cash, too concentrated in one
name or sector, doesn't pay enough for the risk, wrong timing), it's rejected, automatically, with no
exceptions. This layer is why the system can be trusted not to do something reckless even if every
other layer above it made a mistake.

### Claude's second opinion

**How it works.** The handful of top candidates that survive the rulebook get sent to a language
model (production runs a local model, `qwen3.5:4b` via Ollama, since this deployment has no `claude -p`
subscription access — the same code path supports the Claude Code CLI too) with the candidate's
numbers, your current portfolio, and reference notes on the ticker. It writes back a structured,
plain-English verdict: a recommendation, confidence, key risks, and assignment considerations. If the
model is unavailable, times out, or returns something unparseable, the system just proceeds without
its commentary — nothing waits on it, and nothing it says can add, resize, or block a trade.

**In plain English.** Think of this as a second pair of eyes that writes you a short note — "this
looks fine because X, but watch out for Y" — in normal English instead of a spreadsheet of numbers.
It's advisory only. It cannot open a position, and it cannot stop one the rulebook already approved.

### Telegram and the autonomy ladder

**How it works.** Approved candidates are sent to your phone via a Telegram bot, routed into separate
topic threads (CSPs, covered calls, buy-to-own ideas, account snapshots, general ops) so each stays
readable. How much happens automatically depends on a **four-rung autonomy ladder**:
`OBSERVE` (proposals only, no Approve/Reject buttons at all — the default for a fresh install) →
`MANUAL` (every trade needs your tap) → `WHITELIST` (a list of symbols you trust auto-execute,
everything else still needs your tap) → `FULL` (anything that clears the rulebook auto-executes).
Moving *up* a rung is refused until the account has actually demonstrated it works (at least 20 real
fills, a 60%+ fill rate, and at least one automatic loss-reducing close having genuinely fired);
moving down is always allowed instantly. Closing a losing or winning position automatically is a
**separate** switch, independent of the rung — because reducing risk should never have to wait for
you to be free to check your phone.

**In plain English.** This is the "how much do I trust the robot" dial, and it only turns up when
the system has proven itself, not because someone flipped a switch. At the safest setting it just
shows you ideas. At the most autonomous setting, it can open its own trades — but only ones that
already passed every rule above, and it still can't override you, and it still closes risky positions
on its own regardless of which setting you're on.

### Placing the order — execution

**How it works.** Once approved (by you, or automatically at WHITELIST/FULL), the executor builds a
**limit order at the mid-price** — never a market order — rounded to the correct tick size, qualifies
the contract with IBKR, and re-checks it against the rulebook one final time with a live quote before
sending. In live mode, a second explicit `[CONFIRM LIVE]` tap is required per order. If the order
doesn't fill, an optional (off by default) "repricing" chase can nudge the limit price toward the
market a few times before giving up. Rolls are placed as a single atomic two-leg combo order (close
the old option, open the new one together) so there's no gap where you're holding neither or both.

**In plain English.** This is the hand that actually presses the button — but only after checking one
more time that the deal is still good, using a real-time price. It never uses a "buy/sell at whatever
price" market order, so you're never at the mercy of a bad fill.

### Watching positions all day — the intraday monitor

**How it works.** Separately from the 15-minute scan loop, an event-driven monitor subscribes to live
price ticks on every open position and checks six conditions continuously: **delta drift** (the
option has moved further in-the-money than intended), a **DTE threshold** (7 days to expiry — deep in
the "gamma window" where price swings hit hardest), a **21-DTE management point** (a deliberate
earlier checkpoint where closing, rolling, or holding are all still comfortable options — added
specifically so decisions aren't first made under time pressure), an **IV spike**, **ex-dividend
risk** (for covered calls, since early assignment risk rises around dividend dates), and
**assignment risk** (delta deep enough and expiry close enough that assignment looks likely). Any
trigger sends you an alert card explaining, in prose, what happened and why it matters.

**In plain English.** This is the lookout who doesn't wait for the next 15-minute check-in — it's
watching live, all session, and taps you on the shoulder the moment something about an open position
needs a decision, well before it becomes urgent.

### Rolling a position

**How it works.** Two different economics apply depending on why you're rolling. An **income roll**
(an unchallenged position, just rolled forward for more premium) still has to clear the normal
return-on-capital and yield floors. A **defensive roll** (rescuing a position that's moved
significantly against you) is judged differently — it's allowed to cost a small, bounded amount of
money, as long as the new position meaningfully reduces the delta (how exposed you are to further
adverse movement) and improves your breakeven. Roll *execution* (placing the order automatically)
ships off by default — today rolls are alert-only until the mechanics have been verified on a live
paper account; approving one manually still works end-to-end.

**In plain English.** Rolling means swapping a losing or expiring short option for a different one,
usually further out in time. If the position is fine and you're just harvesting more premium, it has
to still make good economic sense. If the position has gone against you and this is a rescue, the bar
changes to "does this genuinely make things safer" rather than "does this pay enough."

### Managing losses and circuit breakers

**How it works.** Two automatic exits run every cycle, independent of the autonomy rung: a
**profit-take** (buy back a short once 50% of the credit received has been captured) and a
**loss-exit** (once the cost to close reaches a configured multiple — 2× by default — of what you
originally collected: a short on a **leveraged ETF** is bought back, because those can run against
you too fast to manage; every other short is on a name you'd be happy to own, so instead of
locking in the loss the monitor proposes a **defensive roll** — later expiry, strike moved away
from the money — for you to approve). Two account-level circuit breakers sit above individual positions:
a **daily loss halt** (trips if today's *mark-to-market* P&L, not just realized cashflow, falls a
configured percentage of net liquidation) and a **drawdown halt** (trips if net liquidation falls a
configured percentage below its recent high-water mark, catching a slow bleed no single bad day would
trip). Either one automatically engages a persisted `/halt` kill switch that stops all new order
transmission — profit-taking closes still run, since closing risk should never be blocked — until you
send `/resume`.

**In plain English.** This is the seatbelt. Individual trades get closed automatically once they've
made their money or once they've lost too much. And if the whole account has a genuinely bad day or a
slow bleed, everything new stops until a human looks at it — closing existing risk is still allowed,
because getting *out* should never be the thing that's blocked.

### The wheel — campaigns

**How it works.** When a cash-secured put gets assigned, the resulting stock position, any covered
calls later sold against it, subsequent rolls, and the eventual close are all linked into one
**campaign** per symbol — tracking cumulative net premium collected and an **adjusted cost basis**
(the assignment price minus premium already collected per share). That adjusted basis feeds back into
the covered-call gate, so a wheel that's already collected meaningful premium can write strikes below
the raw assignment price without the system reading that as "locking in a loss."

**In plain English.** "The wheel" is a classic options-income pattern: sell a put, get assigned the
stock, sell calls against it, maybe get called away, repeat. This feature tells that whole story as
one thread instead of a pile of disconnected trades, and — importantly — remembers that premium
you've already collected effectively lowers your real cost, even though the broker's raw numbers
don't show that.

### End of day — the daily report

**How it works.** At 4:15 PM ET, a scheduled job pulls the day's option premium cashflow, has the
language model write a short journal-style narrative (interpretation only — it's told not to just
restate the numbers), and sends a summary to Telegram. It also appends today's volatility observation
to the historical IV record (keeping IV rank calculations current), reconciles the outcome ledger
(matching closed trades to what actually happened), and saves a snapshot of positions used the next
day to auto-detect assignments.

**In plain English.** This is the nightly wrap-up: what happened today, in dollars and in words, plus
some quiet housekeeping so tomorrow's numbers stay accurate.

### Learning from outcomes — the fence

**How it works.** Every candidate Claude reviews gets logged — its full signal vector, Claude's
verdict, and what the deterministic rulebook would have done on its own — in an **outcome ledger**.
When a trade closes, a reconciler back-fills what actually happened (expired worthless, closed early,
assigned, not filled) and the realized P&L. Periodically, a read-only analysis buckets scores against
real outcomes to check whether the scoring weights are actually predictive. **None of this can touch
the rulebook, the sizing, or the config that gates real money** — it's enforced by a dedicated test
that fails the build if any of this code ever becomes importable from the trading path. A human reads
the evidence and decides, by hand, whether to adjust `scoring_weights.yaml`. There used to be a
feature that auto-drafted strategy adjustments from this history; it was removed because the signal
it measured is deliberately inert (Claude's opinion never actually changes what trades — so scoring
its "accuracy" doesn't mean much) and needed years of closed trades to be meaningful anyway.

**In plain English.** The system keeps a diary of every recommendation and what actually happened to
it, so that over time you (a person) can look back and ask "was this scoring approach actually right?"
— but there's a hard, tested wall between that diary and the part of the system that risks real money.
Nothing it learns can silently rewrite the rules.

### Backtesting

**How it works.** A standalone, offline simulator replays the covered-call/cash-secured-put
strategies over historical prices — either pricing entries with a fair-value approximation (validates
the mechanics, not the edge) or, more usefully, pricing them from the stock's actual historical
implied-volatility record (so it genuinely measures whether the "sell rich volatility" thesis would
have paid off). It's completely isolated from the live trading path — it imports nothing from, and is
never imported by, the engine or execution code, so nothing it computes can ever influence a real
order.

**In plain English.** This is a flight simulator: "if I'd run this strategy over the last year on this
stock, roughly how would it have done?" It's a research tool, kept firmly separate from anything that
can actually place a trade.

---

## Folder-by-folder guide

### `config/` — Tunable settings

These YAML files control how the system behaves. **You change behavior here, not in code.**

**Private vs. committed.** `settings.yaml`, `risk_limits.yaml`, `scoring_weights.yaml` and
`universe.yaml` are the operator's own files and are **git-ignored**; the repo commits
`<name>.example.yaml` beside each (the shipped defaults). `src/common/config.py::config_path`
reads the private file, falls back to the example with a warning when it is missing, and always
reads the example when `IBKR_CONFIG_USE_EXAMPLES=1` (set by `tests/conftest.py`, so the suite never
depends on one operator's settings). A new config key is added to the **example** file (that is
what ships) and given a code default. The table below names the files by their private names.

| File | What it controls |
|---|
| `settings.yaml` | IBKR connection (host, ports, client IDs), scan timing, execution timeouts, logging. The `claude` section also carries `backtest_in_prompt` (**C11**, default `false`) — whether a per-candidate on-demand backtest (`backtest/on_demand.py`) is injected into the strategist prompt as enrichment (off by default because it adds a yfinance fetch per candidate at prompt-build time) — and the N3 headless-subprocess hardening keys: `max_turns` (default `1`, a single agentic turn), `model` (pin the enrichment model id, e.g. `claude-sonnet-4-6`), and `disallowed_tools` (space-separated `--disallowedTools` denylist) so the unattended CLI can't drive tools or the filesystem. The local-LLM keys select and configure the Ollama backend: `backend` (`"cli"` code default, `"ollama"`, or `"cli_then_ollama"` — **this deployment runs `"ollama"`**, since `claude -p` isn't available), `ollama_host` (default `http://localhost:11434`), `ollama_model` (`qwen3.5:4b` here, Task 10 — see `SETUP.md` §14 "Model choice" for the evaluated alternatives and why), `ollama_timeout_seconds` (default `180`, raised from `120` in Task 10 fix round 1 against a measured cold-load wall time of 110-118s on the production-shaped 10-candidate prompt), `ollama_num_ctx` (default `24576`, raised from `16384` in the same fix round — the requested context window; must cover the whole prompt *plus* the generated JSON or Ollama silently truncates one or both, dropping reviews; the production-shaped prompt measured `prompt_eval_count` 12,461 + up to ~2,630 generated tokens, within ~20% of the old 16384 ceiling), `ollama_keep_alive` (default `2m`, lowered from `10m` in the same fix round — the scan loop's 15-min cycle interval already exceeds any keep_alive under 15m, so the scan loop's primary review call was *already* cold-loading every cycle; see `settings.yaml`'s comment block for the roll/EOD/single-ticker cadence check that ruled out a real warm-call benefit worth keeping), and `ollama_temperature` (default `0.2`). **Task 11 — news-grounded review:** `tool_research_enabled` (default `true`) is the single switch for the whole feature (see `src/claude/news_context.py`/`ollama_tools.py`) — `false` fully reverts to Task 10's single-shot behaviour, no NEWS block, no research turn; `news_per_symbol` (default `5`), `news_days` (default `7`), and `news_max_items` (default `25`) size the strategist prompt's `=== NEWS ===` block; `max_tool_rounds` (default `2`) bounds how many `search_news` tool calls the model may make in the bounded research turn; `tool_research_timeout_seconds` (default `60`) is the client-side timeout for that round's `/api/chat` POST. `claude.news_fetch_budget_seconds` (default `30`) and `claude.review_min_call_seconds` (default `60`) — final review I2 — are the NEWS fetch's share of, and the per-call floor under, the review's one shared deadline `tool_research_timeout_seconds + ollama_timeout_seconds`; `market_data.chain_fetch_budget_seconds` (now `350`, was `600`) is sized against that deadline + floor — the arithmetic is in `settings.yaml` and `How the scan works.md` §5b. The `scheduler.entry_cutoff` key (default `"15:00"`) sets the ET time after which the intraday loop stops surfacing new entries (profit-take checks still run until the close). `scheduler.eod_timeout_minutes` (default `60`, Task 4b) is a hard ceiling on a single `scripts.run_eod` run, enforced by `scripts/start.py`'s supervisor loop (`_supervise_eod`/`_eod_tick`), not by `run_eod` itself: a run still alive past this many minutes is SIGTERMed, then SIGKILLed after `STOP_GRACE_SECONDS`, freeing the slot so a later trading day's EOD isn't blocked forever — before this, the launcher only ever spawned a new EOD once `eod_proc.poll()` was not `None`, so one hung run (e.g. an account-summary fetch stuck looping through IBKR's 1100/1102 connectivity flaps) blocked every later one indefinitely (2026-09-29: one run was still alive >11h after starting). The `automation` section holds the automated-trading circuit breakers: `max_auto_trades_per_day` (cap on new-exposure entry orders per ET day), `daily_loss_halt_pct` (auto-engage the kill switch when today's mark-to-market P&L, not fill cashflow, drops this % of net liquidation vs. the prior position snapshot — D3), and `drawdown_halt_pct` (auto-engage when net liquidation falls this % below its trailing high-water mark, catching a slow bleed no single day trips). **Task 12** adds `automation.paper_skip_promotion_gate` (code default `false`, set `true` in this deployment's `settings.yaml`) — a paper-only override consumed by `system_settings.promotion_blockers`: when set, a paper-mode process (`Config.is_live` false) skips the autonomy ladder's fill/fill-rate/close evidence gate entirely (with a warning), letting the paper account run at `full` to observe genuine auto-trade behaviour; a live-mode process ignores the flag outright (a different warning) and always runs the full evidence check on any *promotion request* — but that alone does not cover a rung reached earlier on paper and already sitting in the shared `system_settings` DB when a process starts live, which is what `system_settings.enforce_live_autonomy_evidence()` (Task 12 fix round 1, called at `approval_service` startup) closes by re-validating and demoting the *stored* rung on every live start; see the `system_settings.py` row below and `STATUS.md`'s Task 12 fix-round-1 entry for the corrected, complete picture. Must be `false` before `LIVE_TRADING=true` (see `STATUS.md`'s live-cutover gate, which also requires `./ibkr autonomy manual` beforehand). The `execution.reprice_*` keys configure limit-order chase logic (`reprice_enabled`, default false; `reprice_interval_seconds`; `max_reprices`; `reprice_step_pct`) — when on, an unfilled order is repriced: entry SELLs step toward the bid (floor: `min_live_premium_ratio × approved premium`), buy-to-close BUYs step toward the ask, and roll BAG combos step the net-credit limit toward the live market (ceiling: `min_live_premium_ratio × approved credit`). The `market_data.strike_band_pct` (floor, default `0.15`), `strike_band_iv_mult` (default `1.5`), and `strike_band_max_pct` (cap, default `0.40`, S8) keys drive the IV-scaled strike band (N6): the auto-computed band is clamped to `[strike_band_pct, strike_band_max_pct]` so a high-IV ETF can't explode the qualified-strike/batch count; an explicit per-symbol `universe.yaml → strike_bands` override is not clamped. `monitor.roll_execution_enabled` (code default `false`, **`true` in this deployment's `settings.yaml` since 2026-10-01**, N20) turns roll alerts into approvable roll candidates. `monitor.alert_cooldown_minutes` was removed on 2026-10-01 — the re-alert policy is fixed in `intraday._select_fresh`. **C4** adds `monitor.assignment_alert_delta` (default `0.70`) and `monitor.assignment_alert_dte` (default `21`) — the deep-ITM + DTE thresholds that trigger an assignment-risk alert. **D4** adds `monitor.roll_defensive` (`max_debit: 0.50`, `min_delta_reduction: 0.10`, `require_breakeven_improvement: true`) — the bounded-debit/delta-reduction economics `strategies/rolling.py::generate_roll_candidates` applies when called with `defensive=True` — and `monitor.manage_at_dte` (default `21`, the entry window's floor) — the mechanical close/roll/hold decision point, fired by `monitor/triggers.check_manage_at_dte` well before the 7-day gamma window (`dte_threshold`), while closing, rolling and explicitly holding are all still viable. `intraday.py` passes it into the `limits` dict `check_all` reads, so editing the key changes behaviour (it was defined and triggered on but never threaded through, so every alert silently used the hardcoded 21-day fallback). `market_data.max_concurrent_lines` is now enforced (N15): a `MarketDataCfg` validator fails config load if `chain_batch_size` exceeds it. `market_data.max_strikes_per_symbol` (default `80`, `0` disables) caps how many in-band strikes a symbol keeps (the N nearest spot) before `_build_chain_contracts` builds the cartesian — OTM-side-only per right (calls ≥ spot, puts ≤ spot), not both rights across the whole band — a high-IV name at a wide band with dense ($2.50) spacing could otherwise yield 120+ strikes → a several-hundred-contract `qualifyContractsAsync` burst that floods IBKR with `reqContractDetails` for non-existent weekly strikes and trips a session-wedging pacing lockout (this hung the 2026-06-22 scan on SMH at 768 contracts). `market_data.qualify_timeout_seconds` (default `20`) bounds each qualification chunk: qualification is issued in `chain_batch_size` chunks paced by `request_throttle_seconds`, and a chunk that never resolves returns partial results instead of letting the outer `symbol_timeout` cancel the fetch mid-flight (that cancellation is what left the ib_async pipeline unable to service later symbols). `market_data.symbol_timeout_seconds` (default `90`) bounds how long `/scan` spends fetching one symbol's option chain — if IBKR never responds (pacing violation, error 10197 competing-session lockout), the symbol is skipped and the scan continues instead of hanging indefinitely; on timeout (or any chain error) the orchestrator calls `drain_market_data_lines` to reclaim any market-data lines the cancelled fetch left open against the ~100-line cap. `market_data.spot_history_timeout_seconds` (default `10`) bounds the `reqHistoricalData` fallback inside `_get_spot`/`_get_spot_async`, which is only reached when the snapshot has neither a live tick nor a previous close. The **half-dead-socket guards** (`market_data.health_probe_symbol`, default `SPY`; `health_probe_timeout_seconds`, default `15`; `max_consecutive_chain_timeouts`, default `3`, `0` disables) cover the case where TWS keeps its socket handshake up — so `ib.isConnected()` stays `True` and account summary resolves — but has lost its IBKR data farm (`Error 1100`), making *every* chain request time out; left unguarded, one intraday cycle grinds at `symbol_timeout_seconds` × the whole universe (~115 min) and starves every later 15-min cycle (the 2026-06-24 02:00 SGT stall). Before each intraday cycle `probe_market_data_health` fires one bounded snapshot quote on `health_probe_symbol` and returns a `ProbeHealth` diagnosis (2026-09-09: healthy/unhealthy plus root-cause classification — Error 1100 lost-farm vs 10197 competing-live-session vs no-subscription vs connectivity-flap vs generic — from the IBKR error codes observed during the probe window; see the `market_data.py` row for the full classification and why a 10197 block must NOT be treated as a reconnect-fixable socket failure); mid-scan, `max_consecutive_chain_timeouts` back-to-back timeouts trip a circuit breaker that aborts the run (`ScanResult.aborted_unhealthy`). Either path notifies the operator on Telegram with the diagnosed block reason and forces a reconnect (`ib_scan.disconnect()` → `AutoReconnect`) — except a pre-scan block whose codes include 10197, which instead restarts the IBC-managed Gateway for a fresh login (`gateway_recovery`, `src/ops/gateway_control.py`) and reports the outcome in the message. `market_data.intraday_rescan_move_pct` (default `0.005` = 0.5%) and `force_full_scan_minutes` (default `120`, `0` disables) drive the **S1 intraday materiality gate**: in the 15-min loop an `actively_wheeling` name (`universe.yaml`) is re-fetched only once its live spot drifts past `intraday_rescan_move_pct` from its last fetch; names that cleared the score floor last cycle always fetch; and a symbol in `actively_wheeling` ∪ held is force-fetched once **its own** last fetch exceeds `force_full_scan_minutes` (raised from 90 to 120 on 2026-08-21 — see the `approval_service.py` row below for why a full sweep and the 15-min cadence collide, and the S9 row in STATUS.md for the incident that prompted it). **This staleness check is per symbol (2026-08-27), not "the whole core sweeps together the instant the single stalest name crosses the line"** — that old rule let one quiet name (e.g. MSFT on a slow week) drag every other `actively_wheeling` name into a synchronized burst each time. Per-symbol staleness means a name's clock runs from its own last fetch, so names that move often (and refresh via the move gate anyway) and names that stay quiet naturally desynchronize — periodic refreshes spread out over time on their own, without any explicit batch/rotation schedule. **2026-08-27:** two more thresholds extend the same gate. `market_data.held_position_move_pct` (default `0.02` = 2%, **directional — up only**) replaced what used to be an unconditional "always fetch every held stock position every cycle" rule — previously the single biggest fixed IBKR chain-fetch cost in the loop, since it ignored movement entirely; a held name is now only re-fetched once it has *risen* this much (a new CC candidate needs the extra room a rally creates; a drop doesn't open one, and existing-position risk — delta drift, assignment, rolls — is handled continuously by the separate event-driven monitor, not this gate; a dropping held name is still caught by the per-symbol `force_full_scan_minutes` check above), or has no baseline yet. `market_data.dip_pull_in_pct` (default `0.03` = 3%, **directional — drop only**) governs `would_own` names outside `actively_wheeling` ("dip-watch"): they are not scanned every cycle at all, and are excluded from `force_full_scan_minutes` entirely; they're pulled into a cycle's option-chain scan only once their live spot has *dropped* this much from its last fetch, since a rally is never a CSP entry signal for a name outside the core rotation. Manual `/scan` ignores the whole gate and always sweeps the full universe (dip_watch names seed-only unless gapped ≥3% overnight — see below). **P3-P4 M1 portfolio-spine keys:** `market_data.portfolio_snapshot_interval_minutes` (default 15) — minimum minutes between automatic portfolio snapshots the intraday monitor writes; the gate reads the `portfolio_snapshots` table's newest `captured_at` (not an in-memory clock), so a monitor restart can't burst snapshots, and a `refresh` command ignores it because an operator asking for a fetch gets one; `storage.portfolio_snapshot_retention_days` (default 30) — days of intraday `portfolio_snapshots` history kept, pruned by the EOD run's step 8 beside `purge_old_risk_verdicts` (a year of intraday history needs a rollup, not a longer retention). **The `watchdog:` block (Task 5)** configures `src/ops/watchdog.py`, the out-of-process health check run by launchd/cron: `interval_seconds` (default 300) is how often `python -m scripts.watchdog` should be scheduled to run (the scheduling itself lives outside this repo, in launchd/cron config, not in a Python loop); `heartbeat_max_age_minutes` (default 10) bounds `command_drain_heartbeat` and (RTH-only) `monitor_heartbeat` staleness; `scan_max_age_minutes` (35) / `scan_grace_minutes` (20) bound `intraday_scan_completed` staleness during RTH, mirroring two 15-min loop cycles plus slack and ignoring the first minutes after the open before the loop's first cycle can have landed; `iv_max_stale_trading_days` (3) bounds how many trading sessions old the newest `iv_history` row for any universe∪held symbol may be; `eod_grace_minutes` (90) is added to `scheduler.eod_report` (16:15 ET) to get the time after which `eod_completed` must be dated today in ET; `realert_minutes` (60) is the re-alert cadence while a check keeps failing, for every check except `iv_history`, which instead re-alerts at most once per ET calendar day (an ok→fail transition always sends immediately, a fail→ok transition always sends a recovery notice — and either message is recorded as delivered, and the check's state advanced, only once `send_telegram` actually confirms it sent); `deadman_url` (default `""`) is an optional external dead-man-switch URL (e.g. healthchecks.io) GETed on every run where every check passes — the only mechanism that can notice the Mac itself being off or asleep, since a powered-off machine runs no watchdog process at all. **The `gateway_recovery:` block (2026-10-02)** configures `src/ops/gateway_control.py`, the automatic IB Gateway restart the intraday loop runs when a pre-scan probe is blocked by Error 10197: `enabled` (default `true`), `cooldown_minutes` (30 — minimum gap between restarts), `max_restarts_per_day` (3, per ET day — after that the block message tells the operator to find the other session), `stop_timeout_seconds` (30 — wait for the old Gateway process group before SIGKILL), `port_wait_seconds` (90 — wait for the relaunched API port). Only acts when the `com.ibkr.gateway` launchd agent is loaded. |
| `risk_limits.yaml` | Per-ticker concentration limits, delta ranges, DTE windows, earnings blackout, minimum return. The `live_execution` section adds the send-time re-gate knobs: `min_live_premium_ratio` (reject a fill when the live mid has collapsed below this fraction of the approved premium — F2) and `require_ibkr_greeks_when_live` (in LIVE mode, require IBKR-sourced greeks for the delta gate — F6). `liquidity.morning_volume_cutoff_et` (default 10:30, N19) is the ET time before which the day-volume gate is skipped. `covered_call.min_strike_vs_basis` (default 1.00, N18) blocks below-cost-basis call writes — lower it to allow them on underwater holdings. **C1 IV/RV gate:** `iv.min_iv_rv_ratio` (default 1.05) — reject candidates where `current_iv / realized_vol < threshold`; `iv.realized_vol_window` (default 20 trading days) — the lookback window for the realized-vol denominator in the ratio. Missing ratio is treated as data unavailable and never blocks the scan. **`ideal_zone:` block (strike band + action levels are display/ranking-only; `min_credit_edge_pct` feeds a real gate — see below):** `em_lo_mult_call`/`em_lo_mult_put`/`em_hi_mult` (0.30/0.18/1.20) place the ideal strike band in expected moves — both CC and CSP screen 0.20–0.35Δ, but puts and calls do NOT map to the same sigma distance at equal delta (a 0.20–0.35Δ call sits at ~0.48–1.17σ; a 0.20–0.35Δ put can run as close as ~0.21σ on a high-vol/long-DTE name at the 0.35-delta end), so the **inner edge is split per right** (2026-09-23) — `em_lo_mult_call: 0.30` and `em_lo_mult_put: 0.18` each independently bracket their own right's delta range with margin. A single shared floor can't satisfy both: low enough for the widened put range also drags the CALL inner edge close enough to spot to let a ~0.46-delta covered call read "in zone" (`tests/test_output_fidelity.py::test_ideal_call_band_never_endorses_an_at_the_money_write`, the exact bug this floor exists to prevent) — verified by that test plus the `em_lo_mult_put` sweep in `test_the_band_actually_contains_the_delta_range_the_screen_trades`. `em_hi_mult` (the outer edge) stays shared — 1.20 brackets both rights' outer bound with margin, since that historical bug was inner-edge-only; `earnings_widen_mult` (0.25) pushes it further OTM when earnings fall inside the option's life; `support_pull_pct` (3.0) is how close a support/resistance level must be for the band to snap to it — the snap moves the *outer* edge onto the level and the inner edge is then clamped back to the right's own `em_lo_mult_call`/`em_lo_mult_put`, so the band stretches rather than sliding toward spot (translating it used to drag the inner edge to at-the-money, reporting a ~0.53Δ covered call as "✓ in zone"); `min_credit_edge_pct` (10.0) is the premium demanded over Black-Scholes fair value at *realised* vol — the variance-risk-premium thesis made explicit, and (D2) the number `income.require_vrp_edge` gates on via `TradeCandidate.ideal.min_credit`; `buy_margin_of_safety_pct` (8.0) discounts the analyst mean target when placing the share-acquisition level. Consumed by `analytics/fair_value.py`, which itself never rejects anything — see `risk_engine.py`'s variance-risk-premium gate below for the code that does. **`income:` block:** `require_vrp_edge` (true) is the primary income gate — reject when `candidate.premium < candidate.ideal.min_credit` (reason `premium_below_fair_value`; a missing ideal zone is data-unavailable, never a rejection); `min_roc_pct` (0.15) / `min_annualized_yield_pct` (0.0) are now noise floors only — they used to be the primary gate, and `max(1.0%, 12% × DTE/365)` was a hidden ~25–30% IV floor that made every low-vol name (SPY, GLD, TLT, sector ETFs) permanently unreachable regardless of the actual variance-risk-premium edge (D2). **`portfolio:` block (D1, Task 9 — replaces the single `max_pct_per_ticker` that used to answer three unrelated questions at once, which is why any CSP above a ~$150 strike was unreachable at $300k NLV):** three constraints, each in its own unit, resolved by `engine/capital.resolve_caps` into a `Caps` object for one account snapshot. **Job 1, feasibility (cash):** `cash_reserve_pct` (20.0) and `cash_reserve_absolute` (10000) — the reserve held free at all times is the *greater* of a percentage of available cash (`account.excess_liquidity`) and the absolute floor; `max_csp_allocation_pct_of_deployable` (100.0) is total CSP collateral as a percentage of what's left *after* the reserve ("deployable cash"), not of gross cash or net liq. **The order of application is fixed in code, not configurable:** `deployable = available_cash − reserve` first, then `csp_budget = deployable × max_csp_allocation_pct_of_deployable`; expressing both against gross cash would let the two knobs contradict each other (the bug this replaces — the old `max_csp_allocation_pct: 60` was measured against net liq while only ~$100k of cash existed, so the budget could exceed the money). At $100k cash the reserve is `max(100000×0.20, 10000) = 20000` either way, but at a smaller account the absolute floor dominates and is *not* inert — e.g. at $16k cash, `max(16000×0.20, 10000) = 10000` (the 20% alone would have been $3,200). **Job 2, concentration (risk units — `collateral × IV × sqrt(DTE/365)`, so share price is not a risk measure):** `max_risk_units_per_ticker_pct` (5.0) and `max_risk_units_per_sector_pct` (25.0), both a percentage of net liq. **CALIBRATION WARNING (kept verbatim from the config comment):** risk units are collateral scaled by IV × √(DTE/365), typically 0.05–0.30, so these percentages are NOT comparable to the old collateral-based ones — 5.0 here is far more permissive than 5.0 was before. `max_risk_units_per_ticker_pct: 5.0` was examined, not assumed: `scripts/capacity_report.py` at $300k NLV / $100k cash shows AMD's single lot alone costs 3.06% of NLV in risk units (SMH 2.85%, NBIS 1.89%, QQQ 1.78%, META 1.77%) — the floor below which AMD becomes unreachable again — leaving only a 3.1–5.0 viable range; the human ruling kept 5.0, the permissive end. `max_collateral_per_ticker_pct` (10.0) is the fallback cap used when IV is unavailable, applied to raw collateral (strictly more conservative than the risk-unit cap) so a missing IV can never make a position *less* constrained. **Job 3, deliberateness (explicit, counted slots):** `max_large_positions` (2, raised from 1 on 2026-09-23) and `max_pct_per_ticker_large` (25.0, raw collateral % of net liq per slot — two slots means up to 50% of net liq combined across both names, not a change to either one's own ceiling) — **cumulative** per-ticker collateral (what the account already holds in the name plus the new lot) above `max_collateral_per_ticker_pct` needs a free large-position slot and must still sit under this hard ceiling; without it, a high-priced name is either impossible or unbounded, and one number can't express a third option. Because it is cumulative, this pair doubles as the raw-collateral backstop wherever the risk-unit tallies can't be seeded from live IV (the order-approval re-gate, the single-ticker deep-dive, the CSP generator's sizer — none of which pass `iv_by_symbol`/`iv_of`): whatever those paths know about the candidate's own IV, one ticker can never exceed `max_pct_per_ticker_large` in raw collateral. `max_margin_usage_pct` (50.0) and `max_new_positions_per_run` (10) are unchanged. Both `strategies/cash_secured_put.py` (`screen_csp_candidates`) and `risk_engine.validate_candidates` call the same `capital.max_contracts` helper, so the generator and the gate can never disagree about how big a position may be (the root cause of D1). Verify the effect of any change here with `python -m scripts.capacity_report` (see `SETUP.md`) — after Task 9 formalized these keys into config, 46/46 `would_own` symbols remained tradeable at $300k/$100k, unchanged from the in-code defaults `resolve_caps` had been using. |
| `universe.yaml` | Your watchlist (tickers to scan for CCs) and `would_own` list (stocks OK to be assigned via CSPs). **2026-08-27 restructure:** `would_own` is now split by an `actively_wheeling:` key — a subset of `would_own` (GOOGL/NVDA/AMZN/MAGS/BAC/META/PLTR/HOOD/SOFI/HIMS/NBIS/ASTS/RKLB/RGTI/MARA/TQQQ/UPRO/SOXL/DPST) that's scanned every intraday cycle the way all of `would_own` used to be. Everything else in `would_own` ("dip-watch": SPY/QQQ/IWM/SMH/HACK/IGV, sector ETFs XLV/XLP/TLT, plus AAPL/MSFT/JPM/V/WMT/TSLA/BABA/UBER/CRWD) is would_own-eligible but not part of the automatic rotation. **2026-08-28 trim:** GLD/XLF/XLK/XLE/XLU/XLI/SLV/MA/HD/AMD/NET/SNOW/TTD/DDOG archived out of `would_own` entirely (see `config/universe_archive.yaml`) — realistically won't-trade ETFs, one payments-network name kept (V over MA), AMD dropped for SMH/SOXL instead, and software names the user isn't familiar enough with — see the `settings.yaml` row above for the `dip_pull_in_pct`/`held_position_move_pct` mechanics this drives in `src/orchestrator/scan.py::_compute_material_symbols`. **2026-09-29 addition (operator-confirmed):** AMD un-archived back into `watchlist:` (sector `semis`) as CC-only — held 300sh, deliberately not added to `would_own` (no CSPs); BAC added to `watchlist:` (sector `financials`) *and* `would_own`/`actively_wheeling` — held 1000sh, CC+CSP; DPST reclassified from the CC-only leveraged set into the deliberate `would_own`/`actively_wheeling` exception, joining TQQQ/UPRO/SOXL. Tickers are also organised into three risk tiers for reference (safe bets / moderate / risky — organisational only, orthogonal to the `actively_wheeling` split). Leveraged ETFs are CC-only, never `would_own`, **except TQQQ/UPRO/SOXL/DPST** — a deliberate, confirmed exception where the account accepts daily-reset decay risk on assignment for those four specifically (TQQQ/UPRO/SOXL confirmed 2026-08-27; DPST confirmed 2026-09-29); LABU/TSLL remain the hard "never" case. The `leveraged_etfs:` key (M5, 2026-09-04) lists all six once so `src/research/checks/warnings.py` can derive the CC-only set (`leveraged_etfs − would_own`) and the deliberate-exception set (`leveraged_etfs ∩ would_own`) without a second hard-coded copy. The `sectors:` map groups every name for the per-sector concentration cap. An optional `strike_bands:` map sets per-symbol ±band overrides for the chain scanner (N6) — used for extreme-IV leveraged ETFs (including TQQQ/UPRO now) so their ~0.25-delta strike is in scope even before IV history exists. Tickers ruled out entirely live in the sibling `config/universe_archive.yaml` — reference-only, never loaded by the application (`config.py` only reads the four named config files); see that file's header for how to restore one. |
| `scoring_weights.yaml` | How much weight IV rank, technicals, fundamentals, and liquidity each get when ranking candidates. Also `min_candidate_score` (option-candidate floor) and a `buy_to_own:` block (`min_score`, `max_candidates`) governing the buy-to-own screen. **`zone_fit` (default 0.0 = off):** *not* a block weight — it is a blend **inside** `technical`, mixing regime alignment with how well the strike sits in the ideal band (`fair_value.zone_fit_score`). `engine/scoring.py` excludes it from the block-weight normalization sum (`_NON_COMPONENT_KEYS`) so raising it can't silently dilute every real weight. At 0.0 scoring is bit-identical to before the ideal-zone feature. **`relative_strength` (default 0.0 = off, modernization Phase 3):** unlike `zone_fit`, this *is* an ordinary top-level block weight — `engine/scoring.py::score_candidates` adds `ScoreCard.relative_strength × w["relative_strength"]` straight into `blended_score` alongside `sentiment`/`liquidity`/`assignment_risk`. See `STATUS.md`'s "Phase classification + relative strength" entry for what the underlying number actually measures (not RS vs. SPY, despite the name). **`missing_iv_rank_score` (default 50, top-level key alongside `min_candidate_score` — not per-strategy, since `cash_secured_put.py`, `covered_call.py`, and `rolling.py` all read the same `get_config().weights.get("missing_iv_rank_score", 50.0)`, Task 9, R7; the roll generator's read was added in fix round 1):** the `iv_score` a candidate gets when `iv_stats.iv_rank is None` (no/flat `iv_history`) — never when `iv_rank` is a real `0.0`, which still scores 0. A missing IV rank already passes the IV gate as "data unavailable" (`risk_engine`'s `min_iv_rank` gate never rejects on `None`); scoring it 0 under IV's 30% block weight contradicted that and silently sank TQQQ/UPRO/MAGS/BAC when their `iv_history` went thin — see `STATUS.md`'s Task 9 entry and "How the scan works.md" §10 for the arithmetic. All three generators construct the `TradeCandidate` with `rationale_tags=["iv_rank_unavailable"]` in this case; the CSP/CC tag is merged, not overwritten, by `decision_engine.select_top_candidates_detailed`'s `_build_tags` before Claude/Telegram sees it. `rolling.py`'s roll candidates never pass through `select_top_candidates_detailed` or `score_candidates` (rolls sort by `roc_pct` and skip the score floor entirely — see `risk_engine.py`'s row below), so its tag and neutral `iv_score` are display-only on the roll card, not a ranking or gating change. Set to `0` here to restore the pre-Task-9 behaviour. |
| `research_checks.yaml` | **M5 (2026-09-04).** The check catalogue — 40 checks across 7 categories (value/growth/past/health/dividend for stocks, fund for ETFs, options for both). Each check names a registered metric function (`fn`), a comparison (`op`: lt/lte/gt/gte/between/truthy), a `threshold`, a question-form `statement`, the `requires` inputs that must be present (any missing → `UNKNOWN`, never `FAIL`), and `applies_to` (`all`/`stock`/`etf`). Thresholds are deliberate and published: the reader sees the actual value next to the bar it was measured against, and each carries its reasoning in a YAML comment. Loaded by `src/research/checks/definitions.py::load_checks` (cached). |
| `settings.yaml → ledger:` | **Trade ledger tunables** (docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-05-trade-ledger.md, Tasks 1-12; `LedgerCfg`, `src/common/config.py`). `account` (default `""`) pins the IBKR account the ledger tracks; empty locks it to the first CSV/Flex import's account (R8 — see `CLAUDE.md`'s account-lock note; the spec's original R8 named `IBKR_ACCOUNT`, amended because that's the paper account in v1, not the real one). `live_sweep_minutes` (default 5) is the RTH-only `reqExecutions` sweep cadence inside the approval service (the `commissionReportEvent` hook itself is unaffected by RTH). `sheets_min_interval_seconds` (default 60) throttles the Google Sheet full-tab rewrite. `sheets_tabs` (default `{}`) maps the roles `options`/`buy_and_hold`/`tickers`/`summary` to the operator's existing tab gids (validated: known roles, distinct gids); empty keeps the legacy three `(auto)` tabs. `upload_max_bytes` (default 5 MiB) is the server-side cap on a `ledger_import` CSV payload, read fresh on every validation (`LedgerImportPayload._enforce_upload_limit`) — lowering it actually narrows what `POST /commands` accepts. `flex_poll_timeout_seconds`/`flex_poll_interval_seconds` (default 600/10) bound `fetch_statement`'s SendRequest→GetStatement poll loop. `fx_max_gap_days` (default 7) is the nearest-FX-rate lookup window for the USD summary. Secrets live in `.env`, never here: `IBKR_FLEX_TOKEN`/`IBKR_FLEX_QUERY_ID` (the Flex Web Service), `GOOGLE_SHEETS_CREDENTIALS_PATH`/`LEDGER_SHEET_ID` (the Sheets mirror) — all four optional; the ledger degrades to CSV/live-only ingestion with no Flex pull and no Sheets mirror when unset, never an error. |
| `spreads.yaml` (private) / `spreads.example.yaml` (committed) | **Daily credit spreads — in progress** (docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-07-daily-credit-spreads.md; Task 1 of 17 built, see the plan's Progress log). Typed as `SpreadsCfg` in `src/common/config.py`; ships `enabled: false`, `mode: shadow`, `underlying: SPY`. Only the isolation checks are live so far, enforced at every config load: `book_underlyings` (`[SPY, SPX, XSP]`) may never appear in `universe.yaml`, and with `enabled: true` `market_data.chain_batch_size + max_market_data_lines` must stay ≤ 95 and `ibkr.client_ids.spreads` (30) must exist. Every other key (`schedule`, `entry`, `gex`, `selection`, `risk`, `exits`, `execution`, `backtest`) is read by the `src/spreads/` package the later tasks add. A missing private file falls back to the example. |
| `symbol_directory_overrides.yaml` | **2026-09-13.** A hand-maintained `overrides:` list of `{symbol, name, exchange, is_etf}` for tickers SEC EDGAR's directory omits entirely — verified directly against both `company_tickers.json` and `company_tickers_exchange.json`, neither lists `TQQQ`/`SOXL`/`UPRO` (share classes of a shared trust CIK, not individually-registered filers), which is why the web research console's `GET /research/search` couldn't find them despite all three being part of the live scan universe (`universe.yaml → leveraged_etfs`). No `cik` field — there isn't one to give them. Loaded by `src/research/ingest/symbols.py::refresh_symbol_directory`, which seeds a row only when the symbol has no directory row yet (never overwrites EDGAR-sourced data). Read directly with `yaml.safe_load`, not through `get_config()` — it's a data supplement, not a tunable. |

**Rule:** Secrets (passwords, tokens) never go in these files. They go in `.env`.

### `src/ibkr/` — The IBKR connection layer

Handles everything that talks directly to Interactive Brokers via the `ib_async` library.

| File | What it does |
|---|
| `connection.py` | Opens and manages the connection to TWS/Gateway; enforces one connection per process. Provides a sync (`connect`) and async (`connect_async` / `async with`) connect with backoff, plus `AutoReconnect` — attached to the long-running daemons (approval service, monitor) it listens on `disconnectedEvent` and reconnects with capped backoff (re-subscribing market data on the monitor) so a TWS drop doesn't silently kill them. After its 20 fast retries it logs CRITICAL once and drops to a watch mode — a bare TCP probe (`_port_open`) of the Gateway port every 60s, reconnecting when it answers — so it never gives up on a Gateway that is down for hours. `connect_with_retry(ib, …, label=)` is a shared startup-connect helper: `connectAsync` wrapped in bounded exponential backoff (reuses `ibkr.reconnect.*`) so a fast stop→start cycle, where IB Gateway still holds the prior session's clientId (`Error 326` "client id is already in use"), self-heals instead of permanently disabling that connection (the 2026-06-24 03:42 case where `/account`/`/status` went dark). The approval service's exec + scan connections and the monitor connect through it. |
| `market_data.py` | Fetches live quotes, option chains, Greeks (delta/theta/IV), and historical IV data. Provides both a sync API and an `async` chain fetcher (`get_option_chain_quotes_async`) — the orchestrator uses the async one so every IBKR call runs on the `ib_async` event-loop thread (never a worker thread), respecting the line-limit batching. The strike band is **IV-scaled** (N6): `_strike_band_pct` returns `max(strike_band_pct, strike_band_iv_mult · IV · √(DTE/365))` from the symbol's latest stored IV (or a `universe.yaml → strike_bands` per-symbol override), so high-IV names widen enough to surface their ~0.25-delta strike instead of being clipped by a fixed ±15%. The auto-computed band is capped at `strike_band_max_pct` (S8) so an extreme-IV ETF can't generate a runaway strike/batch count; an explicit per-symbol override is exempt from the cap. **Qualification-storm guard:** after band-filtering, `_cap_strikes` keeps at most `max_strikes_per_symbol` strikes (nearest spot), and `_build_chain_contracts` then builds the cartesian **OTM-side-only per right** — calls only at strikes ≥ spot, puts only at strikes ≤ spot — instead of both rights across the whole band, since `covered_call.py`/`cash_secured_put.py` only ever keep OTM contracts (delta 0.20–0.35, both strategies alike as of 2026-09-23) and would discard the ITM half anyway; this roughly halves the qualify/quote batch count and wall-clock fetch time per symbol versus the old `strikes × expirations × 2` cartesian. `qualify_options_async` (in `contracts.py`) then qualifies that cartesian in `chain_batch_size` **chunks**, paced by `request_throttle_seconds`, each bounded by `qualify_timeout_seconds` — a single 768-contract `qualifyContractsAsync` burst of mostly-nonexistent weekly strikes is what flooded IBKR with `reqContractDetails`, tripped a pacing lockout, and (when the outer `symbol_timeout` then cancelled it mid-flight) wedged the whole ib_async session on 2026-06-22. **Line-leak guard:** every `reqMktData` line (option batches *and* spot snapshots) is opened/closed through `_open_line`/`_close_line`, which register the line in a module-level `_OPEN_LINES` table; `drain_market_data_lines(ib)` cancels every still-open line and is called by the orchestrator after any symbol times out or errors, so a fetch interrupted between opening and cancelling a line can't leak it against the ~100-line cap. **Half-dead-socket probe:** `probe_market_data_health(ib)` fires one fully-bounded snapshot quote on `health_probe_symbol` (qualify + tick-wait both timeout-capped, line always reclaimed) and returns a `ProbeHealth` record — `healthy=False` plus a `diagnosis`, an `action_hint`, and the IBKR error codes observed during the probe window (it hooks `ib.errorEvent` for the duration, since 1100/10197 arrive as events, not exceptions). The intraday loop calls it before each cycle because `ib.isConnected()` can't distinguish a live farm from a half-dead socket whose data requests silently never tick. **Diagnosis classification (2026-09-09):** the old probe collapsed every failure into "half-dead socket / Error 1100"; it now distinguishes Error 1100 (Gateway lost its upstream link — the original half-dead case), Error 10197 (competing live session: another login on the account holds the live-data entitlement, where a reconnect does nothing and the fix is closing the other session or using a second username), Errors 354/10089-10091 (no market-data subscription — an entitlement/config problem, not connectivity), 1101/1102 flaps (link being rebuilt — transient), and the codeless generic case (farm unreachable, nothing observed). The 🛑 *Scan blocked* Telegram message carries the diagnosis, the observed codes, and the matching action hint, so a 10197 block no longer tells the operator to "wait for the next cycle" as if a reconnect could fix it (the 2026-09-08 misdiagnosis). **Scan-efficiency (S3/S7):** the async path centres the strike band on `_resolve_spot_async`, which prefers `price_data.get_ohlcv`'s latest cached daily close (free after the first scan) and only falls back to a dedicated `reqMktData(snapshot=True)` when no cached close exists — skipping a ~2s spot snapshot per symbol per intraday cycle. Per-batch quote waits and the spot-snapshot wait are **event-driven** via `_await_ready`, with the old fixed `quote_sleep_seconds`/2s as a *ceiling*, not a floor. `_quote_ready(ticker, right=...)` waits for both a usable bid/ask **and** that side's open-interest tick (genericTick `101`) before the batch is considered ready — a well-behaved batch still returns early (~0.2–0.5s) once both arrive, otherwise it uses the full ceiling. (Fixed 2026-08-14: OI wasn't originally part of readiness, only bid/ask, so a batch would cancel its line before the OI tick — which streams in after bid/ask — had a chance to arrive; `passes_liquidity_gates` treats a missing OI as an automatic fail, so this was silently rejecting ~97% of quotes as illiquid universe-wide, even on names like AAPL/SPY.) Greeks are still not waited on (absent on delayed/paper data; the Black-Scholes fallback fills them), preserving most of the speedup on the target account. Cancel-between-batches line-cap discipline is unchanged. **Greeks fallback is layered IBKR-first (S2):** `_ticker_to_quote` takes the delta/IV from the first available IBKR per-contract computation (`modelGreeks` → `lastGreeks` → `askGreeks` → `bidGreeks` via `_pick_greeks`) so a momentarily-missing model tick still yields *genuine* IBKR greeks; quotes that have an IBKR IV but no delta are BS-filled locally by `_enrich_greeks_from_ibkr_iv` (no network) — Phase 1 extended this to fill **gamma/theta/vega** alongside delta (skipping only when *all four* are already present); only quotes IBKR could value *neither* greeks nor IV for fall through to the expensive `_enrich_greeks_yf` Yahoo download (now instrumented — it logs how many quotes forced a Yahoo fetch and how long it took). The `_enrich_greeks_yf` path likewise fills gamma/theta/vega from the BS formula after delta. The scan batch requests generic ticks `101,106` (option OI + implied vol) to capture the IBKR IV. Any BS-derived delta is labelled `greeks_source="black_scholes"` (never `"ibkr"`), so the F6 live-execution gate still rejects it as untrusted live greeks. **Fresh tickers (2026-09-30):** `req_fresh_mkt_data(ib, contract, …)` wraps `reqMktData` and drops the connection's cached, idle `Ticker` for that contract first — ib_async keeps one `Ticker` per contract for the life of a connection and `cancelMktData` never clears it, so a re-subscription used to hand back the previous subscription's bid/ask/greeks/OI and every readiness poll passed instantly on stale values. Every short-lived subscribe → read → cancel site (`_open_line`, the executor's `_fetch_quote`/`_refetch_bid_ask`, `roll_executor`, `profit_take`, `approval_service._fetch_pending_prices`, `portfolio` greeks enrichment, the monitor) goes through it; a still-subscribed ticker is left alone. **Black-Scholes spot:** the chain's strike band still centres on the cached prior close (`_resolve_spot_async`), but the BS greeks fallback prices off `_bs_fill_spot_async` — the median IBKR `undPrice` from the chain's own option computations, else a live stock snapshot, else the cached close. |
| `portfolio.py` | Reads your current positions, account balance, buying power, and margin. **`get_positions(ib, *, include_spreads=False)` drops the daily credit-spread book's contracts by default** (`src/common/books.py` — SPY/SPX/XSP), so no wheel consumer (intraday monitor, profit-take, rolls, the scan's budget seeding, the approval re-gate, EOD) ever alerts on, sizes against, or closes a spread leg; only `scripts/healthcheck.py` passes `include_spreads=True` for the account-truth view (enforced by `tests/test_wheel_spreads_isolation.py`). `enrich_positions_with_greeks_async` populates option-position deltas from live model greeks (used by the EOD net-delta-exposure metric, which would otherwise read 0) — **qualifies contracts first** (fixed 2026-09-11, via `contracts.qualify_options_async`): `build_option`'s unqualified contract (`conId=0`) made ib_async's own `Contract.__hash__` raise inside `reqMktData`'s ticker bookkeeping before any network call, so every subscription silently failed and `.delta` was always `None`. |
| `contracts.py` | Builds valid IBKR contract objects for stocks and options; runs `qualifyContracts` to validate them |

---

### `src/data/` — The data abstraction layer

A thin provider abstraction between the analytics layer and the external market-data backends
(yfinance today; FMP/Polygon later). Analytics/strategies/engine never call ``yfinance.*``
directly — they go through the factories here, which read ``config/settings.yaml → data.*``
to pick the active backend and cache the instance process-wide. A future FMP/Polygon swap is a
config change, not a rewrite of every analytics module.

**IBKR is *not* a "provider" here** — it is the broker + execution path (``src/ibkr/``) and stays
untouched. This layer abstracts only the keyless external reads (prices, fundamentals, news
headlines) that the enrichment/deterministic analytics tiers share. The IBKR greeks fallback
in ``src/ibkr/market_data.py`` still calls ``yfinance`` directly and is out of scope by design.

| File | What it does |
|---|
| `protocols.py` | ``typing.Protocol`` classes (structural subtyping) every backend must implement: ``PriceProvider`` (``get_ohlcv``, ``get_last_price``), ``FundamentalsProvider`` (``get_info``, ``get_calendar``), ``NewsProvider`` (``get_headlines``), ``NewsSearchProvider`` (Task 11 — ``search(query, *, days=7, limit=10) -> list[NewsItem]``, keyless free-text search vs. ``NewsProvider``'s per-symbol headlines), ``SymbolDirectoryProvider`` (``list_symbols`` — the full SEC filer directory), ``FilingsProvider`` (``get_company_facts(cik, *, etag=None) -> (dict, str | None)`` — the full XBRL companyfacts document for one filer, ``(payload, etag)``; empty payload on 404/missing-XBRL/304, never raises; on a 304 the input etag is returned so the caller can reuse its cached payload), and ``BulkPriceProvider`` (``get_daily_bars(symbol, lookback_days=400) -> pd.DataFrame`` — ascending daily OHLCV with a ``DatetimeIndex`` for the cold tier, where a keyed per-symbol API would be too expensive; empty frame when unavailable). The market-data Protocols return the *same* shapes the existing yfinance call sites consumed (``pd.DataFrame``, ``dict``, ``list[dict]``) — wrapping the existing code, not redesigning its output. ``SymbolRecord`` (Pydantic) carries ``symbol``, ``cik`` (zero-padded to 10), ``name``, ``exchange``. ``NewsItem`` (Pydantic, Task 11) carries ``id`` (empty from a provider; assigned ``N1``/``N2``/... by ``src.claude.news_context.build_news_block``), ``title``, ``source``, ``published``, ``url``. |
| `factory.py` | ``get_price_provider()`` / ``get_fundamentals_provider()`` / ``get_news_provider()`` / ``get_news_search_provider()`` (Task 11) / ``get_symbol_directory_provider()`` / ``get_filings_provider()`` / ``get_bulk_price_provider()`` factories. Read ``config/settings.yaml → data.*`` to pick the backend, instantiate it, and cache it via ``lru_cache`` for the life of the process. Tests that need to swap a backend monkeypatch the factory (or ``cache_clear()`` it). |
| `yfinance_backend.py` | The active backend: ``YFinancePriceProvider``, ``YFinanceFundamentalsProvider``, ``YFinanceNewsProvider``, and ``YFinanceBulkPriceProvider`` (the cold tier's only backend — the 4.1 licence gate ruled stooq out, see ``docs/web/data-sources.md``; wraps ``YFinancePriceProvider`` so the ``BulkPriceProvider`` Protocol is satisfied). Each method wraps the exact yfinance call the analytics layer used to make directly (``yf.Ticker(...).info`` / ``.history`` / ``.news`` / ``.fast_info``). **No behaviour change** for the pre-M4 callers — the wrapper is the existing code in a class, and ``_period_for_lookback`` still maps every ``lookback_days`` those callers ever pass (≤365) to the same yfinance ``period`` string as before. M4 extends that mapping past 365 days (``BulkPriceProvider``'s documented default is 400) — without the extra buckets, a >365-day request silently fell back to yfinance's ``"1y"`` period and never actually delivered the requested lookback, which is what let the daily-bars ingest (`ingest/prices.py`) accumulate only ~1y of history no matter how many times it ran. The price and news providers are wired to per-provider circuit breakers (see ``breaker.py``). |
| `fmp_backend.py` | FMP (Financial Modeling Prep) stub classes raising ``NotImplementedError``. Documents the swap path; **not wired in Phase 2**. The *interface* is the deliverable — a future FMP/Polygon integration is a new backend implementing the Protocols plus a factory branch. |
| `google_news_backend.py` | **Task 11.** ``GoogleNewsSearchProvider`` — the active (and only) ``NewsSearchProvider``. Keyless: hits ``https://news.google.com/rss/search?q=<query>+when:<days>d&hl=en-US&gl=US&ceid=US:en`` via ``httpx.get`` and parses the RSS with stdlib ``xml.etree.ElementTree`` (no extra dependency). Wrapped in the ``google_news`` circuit breaker (``breaker.py``) plus an **in-process, 15-minute-TTL per-``(query, days)`` cache** (module-level dict, not a DB table — the brief is explicit this stays in-process). ``search`` never raises: a network error, non-2xx, or malformed XML all degrade to ``[]``, logged at DEBUG. Backs ``src/claude/news_context.py``'s ``=== NEWS ===`` prompt block and ``src/claude/ollama_tools.py``'s ``search_news`` tool. |
| `edgar_backend.py` | SEC EDGAR backend: ``EdgarSymbolDirectoryProvider`` (the active ``SymbolDirectoryProvider``), ``EdgarFilingsProvider`` (the active ``FilingsProvider`` — fetches one filer's XBRL companyfacts at ``COMPANYFACTS_URL`` with the same ``EdgarClient`` as the symbol directory, CIK zero-padded to 10, returns ``(payload, etag)``; empty payload on 404/missing-XBRL/304 rather than raising), ``EdgarClient`` (rate-limited, ETag-aware JSON client — never raises on an HTTP failure, degrades to ``(None, etag)`` on a 304/429/5xx instead; retries 403/429/5xx with exponential backoff, honouring ``Retry-After`` capped at 10s; wired to the ``edgar`` circuit breaker so an open circuit returns ``(None, etag)`` without a network call), ``RateLimiter`` (thread-safe minimum-interval limiter, default under SEC's 10 req/s ceiling), and ``user_agent()`` (raises if ``SEC_CONTACT_EMAIL`` is unset — SEC blocks anonymous callers). |
| `breaker.py` | Per-provider circuit breakers (M4). ``CircuitBreaker(name, threshold=3, cooldown_seconds=300.0)`` — consecutive-failure counter (not a rate), states ``closed``/``open``/``half_open``, ``allow()``/``record_success()``/``record_failure()``. ``get_breaker(name)`` returns a process-wide instance from a registry; ``breaker_states()`` snapshots every breaker's state for ``GET /health → providers``. Mirrors the existing ``market_data.max_consecutive_chain_timeouts`` pattern. An open breaker returns the documented empty value (empty dict, empty frame, empty list) **without a network call**, so the affected section renders ``UNAVAILABLE`` while every other section still renders. |

---

### `src/analytics/` — The analysis layer

Computes signals that determine whether a trade is worth taking.

| File | What it calculates |
|---|
| `iv.py` | **IV Rank** (how high is current implied volatility vs. the past year?), **IV Percentile**, term structure slope, put/call skew, **VRP** (Volatility Risk Premium = current IV% − HV30%; positive means options are pricing in more vol than realized, which is the premium seller's edge), and **IV/RV ratio** (`current_iv / realized_vol` over the configured window, C1). HV30 (`_compute_hv30`) derives from the shared `price_data.get_ohlcv` settled-close history. The IV/RV ratio uses `realized_vol.compute_realized_vol` (configurable window, default 20d) and is exposed on `IVStats.iv_rv_ratio`. |
| `realized_vol.py` | **Close-to-close annualized historical volatility** over a configurable window (C1). `compute_realized_vol(symbol, window=20)` reads the same `price_data.get_ohlcv` OHLCV store, computing log-return std × √252 × 100. Returns None when history is insufficient or computation fails — callers treat None as "data unavailable" and never block on it. Used by `iv.py` to compute `IVStats.iv_rv_ratio`. |
| `market_conditions.py` | **The macro backdrop**, fetched once per scan (not per symbol) and returned as a `MarketConditions` schema attached to `ScanResult`. Four keyless reads: **VIX** (`^VIX`) and the **VIX term ratio** (`^VIX/^VIX3M` — above 1 is backwardation, i.e. near-term risk priced above 3-month, exactly when a naive IV-rank screen most misleads a premium seller); the **10-year yield** (`^TNX`, which quotes ×10) with its 5-session change in basis points; the **SPY 5-session return** off the shared OHLCV store; and **broad-market headline tone**, a VADER read over SPY/QQQ headlines reusing `sentiment._fetch_news` so there is one headline pipeline rather than two. `render_macro_context()` emits the `MACRO BACKDROP` prompt block (single-ticker deep-dive only — the full-universe prompt stays compact); `vix_regime()` is the shared 15/20/30 threshold table the prompt uses. VIX/VIX3M are fetched per scan (cheap, intraday-moving); rates, tape, and headlines are `@daily_cached`. Every field degrades to `None` independently. Enrichment only — asserted by `tests/test_eval_skills.py::test_macro_never_reaches_the_engine`. |
| `fair_value.py` | **Where a short option ought to be written, and for how much.** `compute_ideal_zone(...)` returns an `IdealZone`: (1) an **expected move** `spot · IV · √(DTE/365)`; (2) a **strike band** 0.30–1.20σ out for calls / 0.18–1.20σ out for puts (the inner edge is split per right since puts and calls don't map to the same sigma distance at equal delta — see the `risk_limits.yaml` row above), snapped to the symbol's own support/resistance (the first consumer of `TechnicalStats.support_levels`/`resistance_levels`, computed every scan and previously read by nothing), anchored to a 50d/200d SMA inside the band, widened for earnings-inside-DTE and a failed quality screen, nudged up for an ex-div inside a covered call's life, and floored at `cost_basis × min_strike_vs_basis`; (3) a **minimum credit** — Black-Scholes fair value at *realised* vol (HV30) plus `min_credit_edge_pct`, never below what the ROC/annualized-yield gates imply — making the variance-risk-premium thesis explicit. `compute_ideal_zone` prices this at the band's **anchor** strike (the right number for the abstract band); callers holding a specific contract must re-price it with **`zone_for_contract(zone, strike, iv, *, cost_basis)`**, which returns a copy whose `min_credit` is priced at the strike actually on offer and whose ROC/yield component divides by the denominator the strategy's gate really uses (cost basis for a CC, strike for a CSP). Both CC and CSP generators call it per candidate — comparing an offered premium against the anchor's floor mislabelled every strike away from the anchor (a $250C at $2.31, twice its own $1.13 floor, read "below fair value"). The band itself is strike-independent and stays memoized per DTE; (4) **action levels** (the spot at which the anchor sits mid-band — the band's own midpoint multiplier, not a hard 1σ, so a well-placed contract isn't flagged as needing a better entry — and a share-acquisition `buy_below` — the **nearest** level below spot among support, the 50d SMA, the analyst mean target less a margin of safety, and the lower quartile of the 52-week range; anchors at or above spot are discarded, and the deeper ones stay visible in `buy_anchors` ordered nearest-first.
